\displaystyle\phi_{\rm ini}(H)~\simeq~\left(\frac{M_{{\rm Pl}}^{l-3}\sqrt{cH^{2}-m^{2}/2-HM_{{\rm Pl}}y^{2}/2}}{\lambda\sqrt{2l-2}}\right)^{1/(l-2)}.

(D.32)

2.

m_{\rm eff}\gtrsim\sqrt{c}H
: the Hubble-mass becomes sub-dominant and the time-dependent minimum vanishes
\phi_{\rm min}=0
.

3.

m_{\rm eff}>3H
: the Hubble friction drops, the field becomes under-damped,
V^{\prime\prime}(\left<\phi\right>)>9H^{2}
, and starts to roll away from

\phi_{\rm ini}(H\simeq m_{\rm eff})~\simeq~M_{\rm pl}\left(\sqrt{c}\frac{m_{\rm eff}}{\lambda\sqrt{2l-2}M_{\rm pl}}\right)^{1/(l-2)}.

(D.33)

Figure 55:

We numerically integrate the Klein-Gordon equation of motion in the presence of the negative Hubble-induced potential in Eq. (
D.30
), in radiation-dominated universe. There are three stages of field evolution, which depend on the value of
V^{\prime\prime}/H
: 1) critically-damped 2) over-damped 3) under-damped.
Effects from the thermal mass become important for
y>\sqrt{m/M_{{\rm Pl}}}
. They lead to an earlier oscillation and a larger
\phi_{\mathrm{ini}}
, compared to the zero-temperature mass alone.
The field later evolves in a zero-temperature potential at the points marked by the red crosses.
We chose the values
y_{1}=\sqrt{10m/M_{{\rm Pl}}}
, and
y_{2}=10\sqrt{m/M_{{\rm Pl}}}
.

The trajectories shown in Fig. (
55
) assume that the dynamics occurs during a radiation-dominated era.
If the Hubble-induced mass is generated from the inflaton sector, the Hubble-induced mass decreases instead during preheating and the field starts rolling away from the non trivial value in Eq. (
D.33
) as soon as
\textrm{Max}\left[\sqrt{c},\,3\right]H
drops below
m_{\rm eff}
.

Initial angle.

For F-term dominated universe, cf. Eq. (
D.26
), the
U(1)
breaking term receives Hubble-dependent corrections

\displaystyle V_{\cancel{U(1)}}(\Phi)~\simeq~~\left(1+a\frac{H}{m_{32}}\right)\Lambda_{b}^{4}\left[\left(\frac{\Phi}{M_{\rm pl}}\right)^{l}+h.c.\right],\hskip 20.00003pta,\,c=\mathcal{O}(1).

(D.34)

Therefore, the positions of the valleys of minimum potential
\theta_{\rm min}
are time dependent. The same dynamics, just described above for the radial mode, also applies for the angular mode. The angular field rolls towards a temporary vallee
\theta_{0}+\theta_{H}
during inflation when
H_{\rm inf}\gtrsim m_{32}
, and starts rolling towards the Hubble-independent valley
\theta_{0}
when
H
drops below
m_{32}
.
In this work, we simply assume the initial angular amplitude

♠
♠\spadesuit28

♠
♠\spadesuit28

\spadesuit
28

Note that for radiation-dominated universe, the A-term in Eq. (
D.24
), at least in our analysis and in the ones of
[
196
,
216
]
, seems to be Hubble-independent. We leave for further study the question of whether such Hubble-dependent A-term is induced during radiation-domination. In any case, the angular kick could be generated by random fluctuations during inflation, see Sec.
7.5.2
.

\theta_{\textrm{ini}}\sim\mathcal{O}(1).

(D.35)

D.4
Nearly-quadratic potentials

In this appendix, we review the results presented in
[
192
]
which show how spontaneous symmetry breaking can arise due to the running of the soft masses. The authors consider supersymmetric theories coupled to
\mathcal{N}=1
supergravity where
U(1)_{\textrm{PQ}}
is a global symmetry of the superpotential. They show that
U(1)_{\textrm{PQ}}
can be broken by the logarithmic running of renormalization group equations. Their results are not specific of the Peccei-Quinn symmetry and they can be generalized to other global
U(1)
.

Introduce chiral superfields.

The superpotential is given by

\displaystyle W~=~\lambda\bar{Q}Q\phi,

(D.36)

where the
SU(3)_{c}
quantum numbers of the chiral superfields are

\displaystyle Q(\textbf{3}),~\bar{Q}(\bar{\textbf{3}}),~\phi(\textbf{1}),

(D.37)

and are singlets under
SU(2)_{L}
.
The fields transform under the
U(1)_{\textrm{PQ}}
as

\displaystyle(Q,~\bar{Q})~\rightarrow~e^{i\alpha}(Q,~\bar{Q}),\hskip 20.00003pt~\phi~\rightarrow~e^{-2i\alpha}\phi.

(D.38)

There is a continuum of supersymmetric minima

\displaystyle\phi~=~\textrm{undetermined},~Q~=~\bar{Q}~=~0.

(D.39)

SUGRA breaking.

Supergravity couplings lift this flat direction by inducing soft supersymmetric breaking terms characterized by the gravitino mass
m_{3/2}

\displaystyle V_{\textrm{soft}}~=~m_{3/2}A\lambda\bar{Q}Q\phi+\textrm{h.c.}+m_{3/2}^{2}(g_{Q}|Q|^{2}+g_{\bar{Q}}|\bar{Q}|^{2}+g_{\phi}|\phi|^{2}).

(D.40)

The initial conditions for the RGE are set at the Planck scale
M_{\rm pl}
,

\displaystyle g_{Q}~=~g_{\bar{Q}}~=~g_{\phi}~=~1.

(D.41)

The effective scalar potential is

\displaystyle V~=~\sum_{i}\left|\frac{\partial W}{\partial\phi_{i}}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{a}D_{a}^{2}+V_{\textrm{soft}},

(D.42)

and along the supersymmetric minimum
(Q,~\bar{Q})=(0,\,0)
, it is reduced to

\displaystyle V(\phi)~=~m_{3/2}^{2}g_{\phi}|\phi|^{2}.

(D.43)

Renormalization group equations.

Loop corrections to the couplings lead to the running
[
192
]

\displaystyle\frac{de}{dt}~

\displaystyle=~\frac{-2}{16\pi^{2}}e^{3},

(D.44)

\displaystyle\frac{d\lambda}{dt}~

\displaystyle=~\frac{\lambda}{16\pi^{2}}\left(5\lambda^{2}-\frac{16}{3}e^{2}\right),

(D.45)

\displaystyle\frac{dA}{dt}~

\displaystyle=~\frac{5A\lambda^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}e^{3},

(D.46)

\displaystyle\frac{dg_{Q}}{dt}~

\displaystyle=~\frac{dg_{\bar{Q}}}{dt}~=~\frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac{dg_{\phi}}{dt}~=~\frac{\lambda^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\left(g_{Q}+g_{\bar{Q}}+g_{\phi}+A^{2}\right),

(D.47)

where
t\equiv\ln(|\phi|/M_{\rm pl})
and
e
is the gauge-coupling of the
SU(3)_{c}
.
The equation for
e
can be solved analytically with

\displaystyle e^{2}(t)~=~\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{1+\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}t}.

(D.48)

All the couplings
g
are related
g_{Q}=g_{\bar{Q}}=\frac{1}{3}g_{\phi}
, hence we have

\displaystyle\frac{dg_{\phi}}{dt}~=~\frac{\lambda^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\left(5g_{\phi}+3A^{2}\right).

(D.49)

The system of first-order differential equations above has a fixed point at

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}}{e^{2}}~=~\frac{2}{3},

(D.50)

where
g_{\phi}
is running as

\displaystyle\frac{dg_{\phi}}{dt}~=~\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{5g_{\phi}+3A^{2}}{1+\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}t}\right),

(D.51)

and
A
is approximately constant.

Dimensional transmutation.

In the limit
\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\ll 1
, the above equation is solved analytically by

\displaystyle g_{\phi}(\phi)~\simeq~(3A+5)\left(\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{24\pi^{2}}\right)\ln\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{M_{\rm pl}^{2}}\right)+1,

(D.52)

where the approximation
\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\ll 1
is used.
Then we define scale
M_{\textrm{PQ}}
by when the radiative correction changes the sign of effective potential, i.e. at

\displaystyle g_{\phi}(\phi\rightarrow M_{\textrm{PQ}})~=~0~\simeq~(3A+5)\left(\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{24\pi^{2}}\right)\ln\left(\frac{M_{\textrm{PQ}}^{2}}{M_{\rm pl}^{2}}\right)+1,

(D.53)

where
M_{\textrm{PQ}}
is related to
M_{\rm pl}
by

\displaystyle M_{\textrm{PQ}}^{2}~=~M_{\rm pl}^{2}\exp\left[\left(\frac{-1}{3A+5}\right)\frac{24\pi^{2}}{e_{0}^{2}}\right].

(D.54)

With the definition of
M_{\textrm{PQ}}
, the coupling
g_{\phi}
is simplified to

\displaystyle g_{\phi}(\phi)~=~(3A+5)\left(\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{24\pi^{2}}\right)\ln\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{M_{\textrm{PQ}}^{2}}\right).

(D.55)

The effective potential.

The effective potential reads

\displaystyle V_{\textrm{eff}}~=~m_{3/2}^{2}g_{\phi}\phi^{2}~\simeq~m_{3/2}^{2}\phi^{2}\left[\ln\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}\right)-1\right],\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptf_{a}~\equiv~M_{\textrm{PQ}}e^{-1/2}.

(D.56)

For
A=2
and
e_{0}=0.8
, we have
f_{a}\simeq 7\times 10^{10}~\rm GeV
.

Appendix E
Inflationary fluctuations in the rotating complex scalar field model

E.1
Adiabatic curvature perturbations

Any light scalar present during inflation receives quantum fluctuations which classicalize upon horizon exit, e.g.
[
253
]
. When such perturbations re-enter the horizon, they source either the adiabatic or the isocurvature power spectrum according to whether the extra specie thermalizes with the SM or not.
In our model, cf. Sec.
7
, the radial mode of the complex scalar field decays into thermal radiation and therefore contributes to the adiabatic part of the curvature perturbations.
Assuming a quadratic scalar potential with
m_{r}\ll H_{\rm inf}
, the perturbation from the decaying scalar field reads
[
254
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\phi}~=~r_{\mathrm{dec}}^{2}\left(\frac{H_{\mathrm{inf}}}{3\pi\phi_{\mathrm{ini}}}\right)^{2},\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{with}\hskip 10.00002ptr_{\mathrm{dec}}~\simeq~\left(\frac{3\rho_{\phi}}{3\rho_{\phi}+4\rho_{\mathrm{rad}}}\right)_{\mathrm{dec}},

(E.1)

where
r_{\mathrm{dec}}
is the energy density fraction carried by the scalar, evaluated at the time of radial damping.
The upper bound on
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\phi}
corresponds to the value measured by CMB
[
2
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\phi}~<~\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\mathrm{tot}}~\simeq~2.2\times 10^{-9}.

(E.2)

To constrain the model, we consider two limits.

1) Damping before domination.
Assuming the extreme case in which radial damping occurs right after the onset of radial mode oscillation, c.f. Eq. (
7.29
), in order to minimize
r_{\rm dec}
, the adiabatic curvature power spectrum reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\phi}~=~\left(\frac{\phi_{\mathrm{osc}}^{2}}{\phi_{\mathrm{osc}}^{2}+\frac{4}{9}M_{{\rm Pl}}^{2}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{H_{\mathrm{inf}}}{3\pi\phi_{\mathrm{ini}}}\right)^{2},

(E.3)

where
\phi_{\mathrm{osc}}
is the value at oscillation.

•

If the initial field value is set by the stochastic-inflation process, i.e.
\phi_{\mathrm{ini}}^{2}=3H_{\mathrm{ini}}^{4}/(4\pi^{2}m^{2})
in Eq. (
7.33
), the above equation becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\phi}~=~\left(\frac{27}{64\pi^{4}}\right)\left(\frac{H_{\mathrm{inf}}^{6}}{m^{2}M_{{\rm Pl}}^{4}}\right)\left(1+\frac{27H_{\mathrm{inf}}^{4}}{16\pi^{2}m_{r}^{2}M_{{\rm Pl}}^{2}}\right)^{-2}.

(E.4)

In Fig.
56
, we show that the constraints on
H_{\rm inf}
from Eq. (
E.2
) and Eq. (
E.4
) are weaker than the Planck constraints coming from the B-mode non-observation.

•

If the initial field value is driven by the negative-Hubble mass and is stabilized by higher-order terms, the quantum fluctuation of the radial and angular fluctuation are suppressed, see Sec.
E.4
. This is the scenario which we assume in this paper.

2) Damping when dominating.

Assuming
\rho_{\phi}\gg\rho_{\mathrm{rad}}
in Eq. (
E.1
) leads to
r_{\mathrm{dec}}~\simeq~1
and to the adiabatic curvature perturbations
\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\phi}~=~\left(H_{\mathrm{inf}}/3\pi\phi_{\mathrm{ini}}\right)^{2}.

•

If the initial field value is set by the stochastic-inflation process, i.e.
\phi_{\mathrm{ini}}^{2}=3H_{\mathrm{ini}}^{4}/(4\pi^{2}m_{r}^{2})
, the above equation becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}^{\phi}~=~\left(\frac{4}{27}\right)\left(\frac{m_{r}}{H_{\mathrm{inf}}}\right)^{2}.

(E.5)

As shown in light blue in Fig.
56
, the constraint from Eq. (
E.2
) and Eq. (
E.5
) can be quite limiting.

•

As mentioned in the previous paragraph and as we will discuss more precisely in Sec.
E.4
, for scenarios with Hubble-size masses, quantum fluctuations are suppressed and the Planck constraints are avoided.

Figure 56:

Parameter space of a thermalized complex scalar which generates too much adiabatic curvature perturbations. The weakest constraint (
green
) arises when damping occurs at the onset of oscillation. As damping takes place later, the constraint grows and reach
light blue
region when damping takes place at the onset of scalar domination. For the Hubble-induced mass scenario, the green and light blue constraints are avoided due to a large Hubble-sized mass during inflation, see Sec.
7.5
. The red line corresponds to Planck constraints from B modes non-observation
[
2
]
. The gray region corresponds to the requirement of not generating a second inflation era, see Eq. (
8.22
).

E.2
Domain wall problem

Quantum fluctuations of the initial angular phase
\delta\theta_{i}
, and of the initial radial value
\delta\phi_{i}
lead to a fluctuation of the final angular phase
\delta\theta
at the time when the axion potential develops. This can lead to the formation of domain walls (DW). In the absence of CS, e.g. in the pre-inflationary PQ-breaking scenario, the DW are infinite and can not decay, so we must impose
\delta\theta\lesssim\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Assuming
V(\Phi)\propto\Phi^{p}
, from
U(1)
charge conservation in Eq. (
7.19
) and from the scaling
\left<\phi\right>\propto a^{-\frac{6}{2+p}}
in Eq.(
G.41
), we deduce the scaling of the angular velocity

\displaystyle Y_{\theta}~=~\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}~\propto a^{-3}~~~~\Rightarrow~\dot{\theta}~\propto~a^{-3(p-2)/(p+2)}~\propto~t^{-3(p-2)/2(p+2)},

(E.6)

where we assume a radiation-dominated universe.
The angular phase elapsed after the onset of radial mode oscillation which we denote by
t_{i}\simeq m_{\mathrm{eff}}^{-1}(\phi_{i})
reads

\displaystyle\theta~=~\int_{t_{i}}^{t}\dot{\theta}dt^{\prime}~\simeq~\dot{\theta}_{i}t_{i}\left(\frac{\phi_{i}}{\phi}\right)^{(10-p)/6},

(E.7)

The fluctuation in the angular phase can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\theta}{\theta}~=~\frac{\delta\dot{\theta}_{i}}{\dot{\theta}_{i}}+\frac{\delta t_{i}}{t_{i}}+\left(\frac{10-p}{6}\right)\left(\frac{\phi_{i}}{\phi}\right)^{(10-p)/6}\left(\frac{\delta\phi_{i}}{\phi_{i}}\right).

(E.8)

From Eq. (
G.9
),
t_{i}\simeq m_{\mathrm{eff}}^{-1}(\phi_{i})
and Eq. (
7.5
), we can write

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\dot{\theta}_{i}}{\dot{\theta}_{i}}~=~(l-p)\frac{\delta\phi_{i}}{\phi_{i}}+l\cot(l\theta_{i})\delta\theta_{i}+\frac{\delta t_{i}}{t_{i}},\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{\delta t_{i}}{t_{i}}~=~-\frac{\delta\phi_{i}/\phi_{i}}{2\left(1+\log(\phi_{i}/f_{a})\right)}.

(E.9)

The late time phase fluctuation in Eq. (
E.8
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\theta}{\theta}~\simeq~\left[(l-p)-\frac{1}{1+\log(\phi_{i}/f_{a})}+\left(\frac{10-p}{6}\right)\left(\frac{\phi_{i}}{f_{a}}\right)^{(10-p)/6}\right]\left(\frac{\delta\phi_{i}}{\phi_{i}}\right)+l\cot{l\theta_{i}}\delta\theta_{i},

(E.10)

where the last term in the squared bracket dominates for
p<10
and
\phi_{i}\gg f_{a}
.
Plugging the typical standard deviation for a massless field during inflation, e.g.
[
253
]

\delta\phi_{i}=H_{\mathrm{inf}}/2\pi=\phi_{i}\delta\theta_{i},

(E.11)

we obtain the necessary condition for preventing DW formation once the axion potential switches on

\displaystyle\delta\theta~\simeq~\left[\left(\frac{10-p}{6}\right)\left(\frac{\phi_{i}}{f_{a}}\right)^{(10-p)/6}+l\cot(l\theta_{i})\right]\left(\frac{H_{\mathrm{inf}}}{2\pi\phi_{i}}\right)~<~1,

(E.12)

where we replaced
\theta\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
.
The flatter the potential, the slower the redshift of the angular velocity and the larger the final time fluctuation
\delta\theta
.

E.3
Isocurvature perturbations

If the axion contributes to the DM abundance then the quantum fluctuation during inflation in Eq. (
E.11
) generate isocurvature perturbations
[
255
,
256
,
257
,
258
]

♠
♠\spadesuit29

♠
♠\spadesuit29

\spadesuit
29

This problem also arises in the context of the Affleck-Dine Baryogenesis, where the inflationary perturbation leads to the baryonic isocurvature perturbations
[
259
,
260
,
261
,
262
,
263
]
.
whose amplitude is bounded by Planck data
[
2
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm iso}~=~\left\langle\left(\frac{\Omega_{a}}{\Omega_{\rm DM}}\cdot\frac{\delta\Omega_{a}}{\Omega_{a}}\right)^{2}\right\rangle~<~8.69\times 10^{-11}.

(E.13)

For a spinning axion, the axion abundance is set by the kinetic misalignment mechanism
\Omega_{a}\propto\dot{\theta}_{c}
, cf. Eq. (
4.9
). From Eq. (
E.9
) and Eq. (
E.11
), we deduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\rm iso}~=~\left(\frac{\Omega_{a}}{\Omega_{\rm DM}}\right)^{2}\left\langle\left(\frac{\delta\dot{\theta}_{i}}{\dot{\theta}_{i}}\right)^{2}\right\rangle~=~A\left(\frac{\Omega_{a}}{\Omega_{\rm DM}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{H_{\rm inf}}{2\pi\phi_{i}}\right)^{2}~<~8.69\times 10^{-11},

(E.14)

where
A\equiv l-p-\frac{1}{1+\log(\phi_{i}/f_{a})}+l\cot(l\theta_{i})=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

E.4
Solution

A way to cure the three problems listed above - too large adiabatic and isocurvature perturbations and DW overclosure - is to suppress the initial quantum fluctuations by introducing a large mass for both the radial mode
\phi
and the angular mode
\theta
, during inflation
[
264
,
265
,
266
,
267
,
263
,
268
,
180
]
.

As discussed along Eq. (
7.25
), Hubble size masses for
\phi
and
\theta
arise naturally in SUSY scenario where we have

\displaystyle m_{\phi}^{2}~=~\partial^{2}V_{H}/\partial\phi^{2}\simeq~4(l-2)H_{\rm inf}^{2},~~{\rm and}~~m_{\theta}^{2}~=~|\phi|^{-2}\partial^{2}V_{H}/\partial\theta^{2}\simeq~H_{\rm inf}^{2}\sqrt{l^{2}/(l-1)}.

(E.15)

So, in fact, the solution to these problems is built-in in these models.
Quantum fluctuations of massive states are blue-tilted, e.g.
[
253
]
, such that the amplitude of the associated curvature perturbations entering the Hubble horizon long after the end of inflation, are expected to be negligible. This is the scenario which we consider in this paper and therefore we assume that the initial field value
\phi_{\rm i}
is set by the classical minimum of the Hubble-induced SUSY potential in Sec.
7.5.1
and not by the Bunch-Davies quantum distribution in Sec.
7.5.2
.

Appendix F
Damping of the radial motion in the rotating complex scalar field model

F.1
Thermalization

Fermion portal.

We assume that the complex scalar field
\phi
is coupled to heavy fermions
\psi
charged under some (hidden or SM) gauge sector
A_{\mu}
(KSVZ-type interactions)

\mathcal{L}\supset y_{\psi}\phi\psi_{L}^{\dagger}\psi_{R}+h.c.+g\,\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi\,A_{\mu}.

(F.1)

At zero temperature, the scalar condensate decays into fermions pairs with a rate, cf. diagram
a
in Fig.
57

\Gamma_{\phi\to\psi\psi}\simeq\frac{y_{\psi}^{2}}{8\pi}m_{\psi},\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{for}\hskip 10.00002ptm_{\phi}/2~>~\textrm{Max}[y_{\psi}\phi,\,gT].

(F.2)

If the fermion mass, either from vacuum
y_{\psi}\phi
or thermal origin
gT
, is larger than the scalar field mass, then the scalar field cannot excite thermal
\psi
and it is dominantly depleted through scattering with thermally-dressed fermions of the plasma, cf. diagram
b
in Fig.
57
, with a rate
[
193
,
194
]

\Gamma_{\phi\psi_{\rm th}\rightarrow\psi_{\rm th}}\simeq\begin{cases}\frac{y_{\psi}^{2}\alpha T}{2\pi^{2}},\qquad\textrm{for}~\alpha T>y_{\psi}\phi,\vskip 7.11317pt\\
\frac{y_{\psi}^{4}\phi^{2}}{\pi^{2}\alpha T},\qquad\textrm{for}~\alpha T<y_{\psi}\phi<T,\end{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha\equiv\frac{g^{2}}{4\pi}.

(F.3)

The two regimes in Eq. (
F.3
) depend on whether the thermal width
\alpha T
- the typical relaxation rate of the fermion density towards thermal equilibrium - is larger or smaller than the fermion zero-temperature mass
y_{\psi}\phi
.
If the temperature is smaller than the fermion mass,
T<y_{\psi}\phi
, the heavy fermions are absent of the thermal plasma. In that case, the scalar field can decay into gauge bosons through a loop of fermions
[
195
,
197
,
198
]
, cf. diagram
c
in Fig.
57

\Gamma_{\phi\to AA}\simeq\frac{b\alpha^{2}T^{3}}{\phi^{2}},\hskip 20.00003ptb\simeq 0.01,\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{for}~y_{\psi}\phi>T.

(F.4)

For
T<m_{\phi}
, we replace
T
in Eq. (
F.4
) by
m_{\phi}
. The dependence on
y_{\psi}
in Eq. (
F.4
) appears in the logarithmic running of
\alpha
.
So we conclude

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\phi}\simeq\begin{cases}\textrm{ for}~y_{\psi}\phi<T:\begin{cases}\textrm{for}~\alpha T>y_{\psi}\phi,\qquad\frac{y_{\psi}^{2}\alpha T}{2\pi^{2}},\\[5.0pt]
\textrm{for}~\alpha T<y_{\psi}\phi,\qquad\frac{y_{\psi}^{4}\phi^{2}}{\pi^{2}\alpha T},\end{cases}\\[20.00003pt]
\textrm{ for}~y_{\psi}\phi>T:\qquad b\alpha^{2}\frac{\textrm{Max}\left[T,~m_{\phi}\right]^{3}}{\phi^{2}},\\
\end{cases}+\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{y_{\psi}^{2}m_{\phi}}{8\pi}\Theta\left(m_{\phi}/2-\textrm{Max}\left[y_{\psi}\phi,\,gT\right]\right).

(F.5)

Figure 57:

Quantum processes responsible for the thermalization of the coherent scalar field in KSVZ type models. The scattering rates of a, b and c are respectively given by
\frac{y_{\psi}^{2}m_{\phi}}{8\pi}
,
\frac{y_{\psi}^{2}\alpha T}{2\pi^{2}}
and
\frac{b\alpha^{2}T^{3}}{\phi^{2}}
, cf. Eq. (
F.5
). The black blobs in the middle diagram stand for the thermal field corrections to the fermion propagator accounting for plasma effects. The decay width is obtained from the finite-temperature analogue of Cutkosky’s rule
[
269
]
.

The scalar field keeps spinning after thermalization.

The coherent oscillation is a very ordered state and once the oscillation energy begins to be transferred into the thermal bath, the inverse process which creates coherent oscillation is unlikely to occur.
Therefore, the thermalization of the scalar field, when the universe reaches the energy density

\rho_{\rm damp}=3\Gamma^{2}M_{\rm pl}^{2},

(F.6)

transfers all the kinetic energy of the radial mode to the plasma
\dot{\phi}\to 0
.
The kinetic energy of the angular mode, aka the
U(1)
charge, can also be transferred to the thermal bath, e.g. in the form of chiral charges of SM fermions. However, as shown by the supplementary material of
[
38
]
, the creation of an asymmetric fermion abundance costs chemical potential which leads to an energy density
\phi^{4}\dot{\theta}^{2}/T^{2}
and it turns out that it is thermodynamically favorable to keep the
U(1)
charge in the condensate with energy density
\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}
as long as

f_{a}~\gg~T_{\phi\to f_{a}},

(F.7)

where
T_{\phi\to f_{a}}
is the temperature when
\phi
reaches
f_{a}
.
We check that this condition is satisfied in Eq. (
10.24
).
The washout of
U(1)
charge due to chirality flip mediated by Yukawa interactions is shown to be negligible if the temperature of thermalization is smaller than
[
38
]

T_{\rm damp}\lesssim 10^{12}~\rm GeV\left(\frac{\left<\phi\right>}{10^{9}~\rm GeV}\right)^{2}.

(F.8)

Using that the scalar field evolves in its quadratic potential as

\phi=\phi_{\rm ini}(T/T_{\rm osc})^{3/2},\hskip 20.00003ptT_{\rm osc}=g_{*}^{-1/4}\sqrt{m_{r}(\phi_{\rm ini})M_{\rm pl}},

(F.9)

we obtain that the axion rotation is preserved from wash-out from the onset of oscillation until the final stage
\phi\to f_{a}
as long as

f_{a}~>~10^{4}~\textrm{GeV}\left(\frac{m_{r}}{f_{a}}\right)^{3/5}\left(\frac{M_{\rm pl}}{\phi_{\rm ini}}\right)^{4/5}.

(F.10)

The later condition is largely satisfied in the parameter space of our interest, and wash-out never occurs.

Thermal corrections to the potential.

The presence of the interactions in Eq. (
F.1
) generates thermal corrections to the scalar potential
[
193
,
194
]

\displaystyle V(\phi)=m_{r}^{2}\phi^{2}\left(\ln\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}-1\right)+m_{r}^{2}f_{a}^{2}+V_{\rm th}(\phi,\,T),

(F.11)

\displaystyle V_{\rm th}(\phi,\,T)=\frac{1}{2}y_{\psi}^{2}T^{2}\phi^{2}\Theta(T-y_{\psi}\phi)+a\alpha^{2}T^{4}\ln{\lambda_{\phi}^{2}\phi^{2}/T^{2}}\Theta(y_{\psi}\phi-T)\hskip 20.00003pta=\mathcal{O}(1),\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha\equiv\frac{g^{2}}{4\pi}.

(F.12)

The first Heaviside function stands for the Boltzmann suppression of the fermion
\psi
abundance in the thermal plasma in the large vev limit
y_{\psi}\phi\gg T
. In that case the thermal corrections are given by the thermal-log potential, obtained after integrating out the heavy fermions
[
195
,
199
,
197
,
198
]
. At small vev value
\lambda\phi<T
, the running of the gauge coupling constant
g
becomes independent of
\phi
, which explains the second Heaviside function.

Impossibility to generate efficient thermalization when neglecting thermal corrections.

Let’s first assume that thermal corrections to the potential in Eq. (
F.12
) do not impact the scalar field dynamics. This happens whenever the thermal mass of the scalar fields can be neglected at the time of oscillation
T_{\rm osc}\sim\sqrt{m_{\rm r,\,eff}M_{\rm pl}}
, see Eq. (
8.1
),

♠
♠\spadesuit30

♠
♠\spadesuit30

\spadesuit
30

We backward check that this coincides with
y_{\psi}\phi_{\rm ini}\lesssim T_{\rm osc}
(the first Heaviside function in Eq. (
F.12
)) whenever the initial field value
\phi_{\rm ini}
is sub-planckian

\frac{T_{\rm osc}}{y_{\psi}}\gtrsim\frac{T_{\rm osc}^{2}}{m_{\rm r,eff}}\simeq\frac{M_{\rm pl}}{\left(\frac{\pi^{2}g_{*}}{10}\right)^{1/4}}\gtrsim\phi_{\rm ini}.

(F.13)

y_{\psi}T_{\rm osc}\lesssim m_{\rm r,eff},\hskip 10.00002pt\implies\hskip 10.00002pty_{\psi}\lesssim\left(\frac{\pi^{2}g_{*}}{10}\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{\frac{m_{\rm r,eff}}{M_{\rm,pl}}}.

(F.14)

The duration of the kination era reads, cf. Sec.
8.2

e^{N_{\rm KD}}\equiv\frac{a_{\rm Kf}}{a_{\rm Ki}}=\left(\frac{\textrm{Min}\left[\rho_{\rm dom},\,\rho_{\rm damp}\right]}{\rho_{\rm K,i}}\right)^{1/6}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)^{2/3},

(F.15)

with

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm dom}=\frac{27V(\phi_{\rm ini})^{4}}{m_{r,\textrm{eff}}^{6}M_{\rm pl}^{6}},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\rm damp}=3\Gamma^{2}M_{\rm pl}^{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\rm K,i}=\frac{1}{2}f_{a}^{2}m_{r,\textrm{eff}}^{2}.

(F.16)

From plugging

♠
♠\spadesuit31

♠
♠\spadesuit31

\spadesuit
31

We checked that
y_{\psi}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{m_{\rm r,eff}}{M_{\rm,pl}}}
implies
\frac{y_{\psi}\phi_{\rm damp}}{T_{\rm damp}}\lesssim(b\alpha^{2})^{1/3}\left(\frac{m_{r,\textrm{eff}}}{M_{\rm pl}}\right)^{1/6}
and
\frac{T_{\rm damp}}{m_{r,\textrm{eff}}}\lesssim 1
, leading to
\Gamma\simeq\frac{y_{\psi}^{2}m_{\phi}}{8\pi}
in Eq. (
F.5
).

\Gamma\simeq\frac{y_{\psi}^{2}m_{\phi}}{8\pi}
, cf. Eq. (
F.5
),
\phi_{\rm ini}\simeq M_{\rm pl}
, which corresponds to the
l\to\infty
limit of Eq. (
7.28
) and
y_{\psi}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{m_{\rm r,eff}}{M_{\rm,pl}}}
, cf. Eq. (
F.14
) we obtain

e^{N_{\rm KD}}~\lesssim~0.3\,g_{*}^{1/6}\left(\frac{m_{r,\textrm{eff}}(f_{a})}{f_{a}}\right)^{1/3}\epsilon^{2/3}~<~1.

(F.17)

So we conclude that whenever the conditions of neglecting the thermal mass in Eq. (
F.14
) is satisfied, thermalization via fermion portal is not efficient enough to generate a kination era.

For this reason, in scenario I of Sec.
8
, we must postulate the existence of an unknown mechanism, other than thermalization via fermion or Higgs portal, to damp the radial mode.

The effects from the thermal mass are considered in scenario II of Sec.
9
. Unfortunately, the suppression of the angular kick and the delay of the matter domination prevents the onset of kination.

In scenario III in Sec.
10
, instead of choosing a low Yukawa coupling
y_{\psi}
, we get rid of the thermal effects at the onset of the radial mode oscillation by Boltzmann-suppressing the fermion abundance.

Higgs portal.

In the previous paragraph, we have considered the fermion portal.
If instead the scalar condensate thermalizes through a Higgs mixing (DFSZ-type interactions)

\mathcal{L}~\supset~\lambda_{\rm H}|\phi|^{2}H^{\dagger}H.

(F.18)

The thermal mass correction to the condensate is

V_{\rm th}(\phi,\,T)=\lambda_{\rm H}T^{2}\phi^{2}.

(F.19)

The quantum processes responsible for the thermalization of the condensate are described in
[
270
]
.
In contrast to the fermion portal (denoted by
\psi
), in the case of the Higgs portal (denoted by
H
) the thermalization rates scale as the the forth power of the thermal mass. Indeed

\Gamma_{\rm\psi}\propto m_{\rm th,\psi}^{2},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\Gamma_{\rm H}\propto m_{\rm th,H}^{4},

(F.20)

with

m_{\rm th,\psi}^{2}=y_{\psi}^{2}T^{2},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003ptm_{\rm th,H}^{2}=\lambda_{H}^{2}T^{2}.

(F.21)

We conclude that the situation of the previous paragraph - to impose a small thermal mass prevents thermalization to occur before the start of the would-be kination era - is even worse in the case of the Higgs portal.

The larger dependence of the damping rate on the thermal mass in the Higgs portal, in Eq. (
F.20
), is the reason why we focus on the fermion portal in the main text, cf. Sec.
9
and Sec.
10
.

F.2
Parametric resonance

In this paper we focus on the zero-mode dynamics. However, there may be circumstances where higher modes get produced. We now introduce the framework to study early parametric resonance, which may occur in the UV completion and may have important consequences on the whole dynamics.
The equation of motion of a complex scalar reads

\ddot{\Phi}-a^{-2}\nabla^{2}\Phi+3H\dot{\Phi}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial\Phi^{\dagger}}

(F.22)

which after decomposing in polar coordinates
\Phi=\phi e^{i\theta}
, becomes

\displaystyle\ddot{\phi}-a^{-2}\nabla^{2}\phi+3H\dot{\phi}+V^{\prime}(\phi)=\phi\dot{\theta}^{2}-a^{-2}\phi(\nabla{\theta})^{2},

(F.23)

\displaystyle\phi\ddot{\theta}-a^{-2}\phi\nabla^{2}\theta+3H\phi\dot{\theta}=-2\dot{\phi}\dot{\theta}+2a^{-2}\nabla{\phi}\nabla{\theta}.

(F.24)

We can decompose
(\phi(x,\,t),\,\theta(x,\,t))
into the superposition of a classical homogeneous mode
(\phi(t),\,\theta(t))
and small fluctuations
(\delta\phi(x,\,t),\,\delta\theta(x,\,t))
around it

\displaystyle\phi(x,\,t)=\phi(t)+\delta\phi(x,\,t)=\phi(t)+\left(\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}a^{\phi}_{k}\,u^{\phi}_{k}(t)\,e^{ikx}+\textrm{h.c.}\right),

(F.25)

\displaystyle\theta(x,\,t)=\theta(t)+\delta\theta(x,\,t)=\theta(t)+\left(\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}a_{k}^{\theta}\,u_{k}^{\theta}(t)\,e^{ikx}+\textrm{h.c.}\right),

(F.26)

with
a^{i}_{k}
,
a_{k}^{i\dagger}
being the annihilation and creation operators of field
i

\left[a_{k}^{i},\,a_{k^{\prime}}^{j\dagger}\right]=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{ij}\delta^{(3)}(k-k^{{}^{\prime}}),

(F.27)

and the initial condition for the mode function
u_{k}^{i}
at
t\to-\infty
is given by

u_{k}^{i}(t)=\frac{e^{i(k/a)t}}{a\sqrt{2k}}.

(F.28)

We now treat
(\delta\phi(x,\,t),\,\delta\theta(x,\,t))
as perturbations. We expand the potential around the background solution

V^{\prime}(\phi(x,\,t))=V^{\prime}(\phi)+V^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\partial\phi+\frac{1}{2}V^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)\partial\phi\partial\phi,

(F.29)

such that after spatial and quantum averaging
\left<\cdots\right>
we get, e.g.
[
39
,
150
,
271
]
.

\displaystyle\ddot{\phi}+3H\dot{\phi}+V^{\prime}(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}V^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)\left<\delta\phi^{2}\right>=\phi\dot{\theta}^{2}+\phi\left<\delta\dot{\theta}^{2}\right>+2\dot{\theta}\left<\delta\phi\delta\dot{\theta}\right>-2a^{-2}\phi\left<\nabla\delta\theta\nabla\delta\theta\right>,

(F.30)

\displaystyle\phi\ddot{\theta}+3H\phi\dot{\theta}+3H\left<\delta\phi\delta\dot{\theta}\right>=-2\dot{\phi}\dot{\theta}-2\left<\delta\dot{\phi}\delta\dot{\theta}\right>-\left<\delta\phi\delta\ddot{\theta}\right>+2a^{-2}\left<\nabla\delta\phi\nabla\delta\theta\right>,

(F.31)

for the zero mode, and

\displaystyle\delta\ddot{\phi}+3H\delta\dot{\phi}+V^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\delta\phi+\frac{k^{2}}{a^{2}}\delta\phi=\delta\phi\dot{\theta}^{2}+2\phi\dot{\theta}\delta\dot{\theta},

(F.32)

\displaystyle\delta\phi\ddot{\theta}+\phi\delta\ddot{\theta}+3H\delta\phi\dot{\theta}+3H\phi\delta\dot{\theta}+\frac{k^{2}}{a^{2}}\delta\theta=-2\delta\dot{\phi}\dot{\theta}-2\dot{\phi}\delta\dot{\theta},

(F.33)

for the higher modes, where

\left<\delta\phi^{2}\right>=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left|u_{k}^{\phi}\right|^{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\left<\delta\theta^{2}\right>=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left|u_{k}^{\theta}\right|^{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\left<\delta\theta\delta\phi\right>=0.

(F.34)

Since
[\phi,\,\theta]=0
, all the quantum average of cross terms in Eq. (
F.30
) and (
F.31
) vanish and we get

\displaystyle\ddot{\phi}+3H\dot{\phi}+V^{\prime}(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}V^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)\left<\delta\phi^{2}\right>=\phi\dot{\theta}^{2}+\phi\left<\delta\dot{\theta}^{2}\right>-2\phi\left<\frac{k^{2}}{a^{2}}\delta\theta^{2}\right>,

(F.35)

\displaystyle\phi\ddot{\theta}+3H\phi\dot{\theta}=-2\dot{\phi}\dot{\theta},

(F.36)

We conclude that the Noether charge

n_{\theta}=\phi^{2}\dot{\theta},

(F.37)

is conserved during parametric resonance.
Note that from using Eq. (
F.36
), Eq. (
F.33
) simplifies to
[
271
]

\delta\ddot{\theta}+3H\delta\dot{\theta}+\frac{k^{2}}{a^{2}}\delta\theta=-\frac{2\delta\dot{\phi}\dot{\theta}}{\phi}-\frac{2\dot{\phi}\delta\dot{\theta}}{\phi}+\frac{2\dot{\phi}\dot{\theta}}{\phi^{2}}\delta\phi

(F.38)

In order to address the question of whether parametric resonance is a successful mechanism for damping the radial mode
\dot{\phi}\to 0
or not - an important question for scenario I in Sec.
8
- we need to solve the system of Eqs. (
F.32
), (
F.33
), (
F.35
) and (
F.36
). We leave this question for further study.

Appendix G
Detailed solution of field evolution in the rotating complex scalar field model

G.1
The angular kick

The angular EOM, Eq. (
7.17
), can be rewritten as a Boltzmann equation for the
U(1)
charge
n_{\theta}

\displaystyle\dot{n_{\theta}}+3Hn_{\theta}~=~-\frac{\partial V_{\cancel{U(1)}}}{\partial\theta},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002ptn_{\theta}\equiv\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}.

(G.1)

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(a^{3}n_{\theta}\right)~=~-a^{3}\frac{\partial V_{\cancel{U(1)}}}{\partial\theta}~~~\Rightarrow~~~\frac{d}{da}\left(a^{3}n_{\theta}\right)~=~-\frac{a^{2}}{H^{2}}\frac{\partial V_{\cancel{U(1)}}}{\partial\theta}.

(G.2)

This equation suggests that the
U(1)
charge production rate is proportional to the potential gradient in the angular direction.
Numerical simulations plotted in Fig.
26
show that the charge generation can possibly start even before that the field starts to roll in the radial direction at
3H\simeq m_{r,\textrm{eff}}
.
Once the field value
\phi
drops substantially, the explicit breaking term becomes negligible, see Fig.
58
, and the
U(1)
charge
n_{\theta}a^{3}
becomes conserved.

We can determine the angular kick by integrating Eq. (
G.2
)

\displaystyle a^{3}n_{\theta}(a)-a_{i}^{3}n_{\theta}(a_{i})~=~-\int_{a_{i}}^{a}d\tilde{a}~\frac{\tilde{a}^{2}}{H}\frac{\partial V_{\cancel{U(1)}}}{\partial\theta}~=~\int_{a_{i}}^{a}d\tilde{a}~\frac{\tilde{a}^{2}}{H}2l\Lambda_{b}^{4}\left(\frac{\phi}{M}\right)^{l}\sin(l\theta),

(G.3)

where
a_{i}
is the scale factor of the universe in the far past, where we plugged the explicit breaking potential in Eq. (
D.3
).
The production rate of the
U(1)
charge
n_{\theta}
behaves differently accordingly to whether the radial mode
\phi
has started rolling
3H<m_{r,\textrm{eff}}
, or not.

•

Before oscillation:
the field initially stands at
\phi_{\textrm{ini}}
, see Sec.
7.5
.
Assuming the universe is dominated by the background energy density
\rho\propto a^{-q}
, the corresponding charge generation is

\displaystyle a^{3}n_{\theta}(a)-a_{i}^{3}n_{\theta}(a_{i})~

\displaystyle=~2l\Lambda_{b}^{4}\left(\frac{\phi_{\textrm{ini}}}{M}\right)^{l}\int_{a_{i}}^{a}d\tilde{a}~\frac{\tilde{a}^{2}}{H}\sin(l\theta),

(G.4)

\displaystyle\simeq~2l\Lambda_{b}^{4}\left(\frac{\phi_{\textrm{ini}}}{M}\right)^{l}\frac{\sin(l\theta_{\textrm{ini}})}{H_{\textrm{ini}}a_{\textrm{ini}}^{p/2}}\int_{a_{i}}^{a}d\tilde{a}~\tilde{a}^{2+q/2},

(G.5)

\displaystyle a^{3}n_{\theta}(a)-a_{i}^{3}n_{\theta}(a_{i})~

\displaystyle\simeq~2l\Lambda_{b}^{4}\left(\frac{\phi_{\textrm{ini}}}{M}\right)^{l}\frac{\sin(l\theta_{\textrm{ini}})}{H_{\textrm{ini}}a_{\textrm{ini}}^{q/2}}\left(\frac{2}{6+q}\right)\left(a^{6+q}-a_{i}^{6+q}\right)^{1/2},

(G.6)

where we approximate
\sin(l\theta)\sim\mathcal{O}(1)\sim\sin(l\theta_{\textrm{ini}})
and take it out of the integration.
The late-time contribution dominates the charge generation such that we have the
U(1)
charge evolution

\displaystyle n(a)~\simeq~\left(\frac{4l}{6+q}\right)\Lambda_{b}^{4}\left(\frac{\phi_{\textrm{ini}}}{M}\right)^{l}\frac{\sin(l\theta_{\textrm{ini}})}{H},

(G.7)

and we see that the field receives a kick even before the time of oscillation with
\dot{\theta}\propto a^{q/2}
.
The
U(1)
charge is maximally generated at the onset of oscillation

\displaystyle n_{\theta}(a_{\textrm{osc}})~\simeq~\left(\frac{4l}{6+q}\right)\Lambda_{b}^{4}\left(\frac{\phi_{\textrm{ini}}}{M}\right)^{l}\frac{\sin(l\theta_{\textrm{ini}})}{H_{\textrm{osc}}}~\simeq~\left(\frac{12l}{6+q}\right)\Lambda_{b}^{4}\left(\frac{\phi_{\textrm{ini}}}{M}\right)^{l}\frac{\sin(l\theta_{\textrm{ini}})}{m_{r,\textrm{eff}}},

(G.8)

and the corresponding angular velocity reads

\displaystyle\dot{\theta}_{\textrm{osc}}~\simeq~\left(\frac{12l}{6+q}\right)\Lambda_{b}^{4}\left(\frac{\phi_{\textrm{ini}}}{M}\right)^{l}\frac{\sin(l\theta_{\textrm{ini}})}{m_{r,\textrm{eff}}\phi_{\textrm{ini}}^{2}},

(G.9)

•

After the oscillation:
gradients of the potential in angular and radial directions kick the field into an elliptic orbit whose size redshifts over time.
In App.
G.2
, we determine the dynamics of the radial mode as a function of the shape of the
U(1)
-symmetric potential

\displaystyle V(\phi)~\propto~\phi^{p}\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\left<\phi\right>~\propto~a^{-6/(2+p)}.

(G.10)

Assuming that the Hubble factor evolves as
H\propto a^{-q/2}
, then the
U(1)
charge generated after oscillation
3H<m_{r,\textrm{eff}}
, in Eq. (
G.3
), reads

\displaystyle a^{3}n_{\theta}(a)-a_{\textrm{osc}}^{3}n_{\theta}(a_{\textrm{osc}})~

\displaystyle\sim~\int_{a_{\textrm{osc}}}^{a}d\tilde{a}~\tilde{a}^{\frac{(6+q)(2+p)-12l}{2(2+p)}-1}.

(G.11)

Depending on the values of
q
,
p
, and
l
, we obtain three regimes

\displaystyle a^{3}n_{\theta}(a)-a_{\textrm{osc}}^{3}n_{\theta}(a_{\textrm{osc}})~\sim~\begin{cases}a^{\frac{(6+q)(2+p)-12l}{2(2+p)}}~~&\textrm{for}~~l<\frac{(6+q)(2+p)}{12},\\[5.0pt]
\log a~~&\textrm{for}~~l=\frac{(6+q)(2+p)}{12},\\[5.0pt]
a^{-\frac{12l-(6+q)(2+p)}{2(2+p)}}~~&\textrm{for}~~l>\frac{(6+q)(2+p)}{12}.\end{cases}

(G.12)

In the first and second cases, the
U(1)
charge continues to increase after the oscillation, while in the third case at large
l
, the
U(1)
charge stops being efficiently produced after a few Hubble times and the
U(1)
-symmetry is restored.
Assuming a radiation-dominated universe (
q=4
), the estimated
U(1)
-symmetry after the oscillation demands that
l
is greater than
10/3
and
5
for quadratic (
p=2
) and quartic (
p=4
) potentials, respectively.
Hence, in this work we consider cases
l\geq 4
in order to neglect explicit breaking terms at later time.

Figure 58:

For each
l
, we show the ratio of the
U(1)
-violating potential to the
U(1)
-conserving one, assuming nearly-quadratic potential, along the
\theta=0
direction which has the maximum
V_{\cancel{U(1)}}
.
For
l>2
, the conserving potential always dominates at small field values
\phi\ll M
, and the
U(1)
charge, defined in Eq. (
7.19
), is conserved in the subsequent evolution.

G.2
After the kick

The
U(1)
-conserving and the
U(1)
-breaking potentials excite the field in the radial and angular directions, respectively. As argued in App.
G.1
, after a few Hubble times of field evolution, for
l\geq 4
we can neglect effects coming from the
U(1)
-breaking terms.

Exact solution.

We now derive an exact analytical solution of the complex scalar equation of motion in a quadratic potential without Hubble friction, e.g.
[
272
,
273
]
.
For
\phi\gg f_{a}
, the nearly-quadratic potential in Eq. (
7.4
) is approximated to
V=m^{2}\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi/2=m^{2}\phi^{2}/2
, where
m
is a constant or a slowly-changing variable.
The
U(1)
-charge conservation in Eq. (
7.19
), and the radial EOM in Eq. (
7.16
), give

\displaystyle\ddot{\phi}+3H\dot{\phi}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}-\frac{Y^{2}}{\phi^{3}a^{6}}~

\displaystyle=~0,

(G.13)

where
Y\equiv a^{3}\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}=
is the conserved comoving
U(1)
charge.
We use a set of dimensionless parameters

\displaystyle\tau~\equiv~mt,~~h~\equiv~\frac{H}{m},~~u~\equiv~\sqrt{\frac{m}{Y}}a^{3/2}\phi,

(G.14)

which simplifies the charge conservation equation to
\dot{\theta}u^{2}~=~m
and the EOM to

\displaystyle 0~=~u^{\prime\prime}+\left[1-u^{-4}-\frac{3}{2}\left(h^{\prime}+\frac{3}{2}h^{2}\right)\right]u~\simeq~u^{\prime\prime}+u-\frac{1}{u^{3}},

(G.15)

where
\cdots^{\prime}
denotes the derivative w.r.t.
\tau
, and where we considered the oscillation time scale to be much faster than a Hubble time
h\to 0
, which becomes true after oscillation has started
3H\simeq m_{r,\textrm{eff}}
.
The differential equation in Eq. (
G.15
) can be understood as the equation of motion of a scalar
u
evolving in a potential

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u)~=~\frac{u^{2}+u^{-2}}{2},

(G.16)

whose minimum is at
u=1
, or equivalently
\dot{\theta}=m
. Its corresponding constant-of-motion
E_{u}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{dE_{u}}{d\tau}~\equiv~\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\frac{u^{\prime 2}}{2}+\frac{u^{2}+u^{-2}}{2}\right)~=~0,

(G.17)

with
E_{u}\geq 1
.
The parameter
E_{u}
can be related to the energy density of the scalar field

\displaystyle\rho_{\Phi}~=~\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}+\frac{\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}}{2}+V~=~mYE_{u}a^{-3}.

(G.18)

We just found that the energy density of a complex scalar field evolving in a quadratic potential, redshifts as matter
\rho_{\Phi}\propto a^{-3}
.

To find the exact solution to the EOM, we rewrite Eq. (
G.17
) as

\displaystyle u^{\prime 2}~=~2E_{u}-u^{2}-u^{-2}~~~\Rightarrow~~~U^{\prime 2}~=~-4U^{2}+8E_{u}U-4,

(G.19)

where
U\equiv u^{2}
.
Integrating this equation, we deduce

\displaystyle\int dt~=~\int\frac{dU}{\sqrt{-4U^{2}+8E_{u}U-4}}~~~

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~~2(\tau+\psi)~=~-\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{E_{u}-U}{\sqrt{-U^{2}+2E_{u}U-1}}\right),

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~u(\tau)~=~\sqrt{(E_{u}^{2}-1)^{1/2}\sin(2\tau+\psi)+E_{u}}.

(G.20)

where
\psi
is a phase which depends on the initial conditions.
In
u
-space, the orbit is a fixed-sized ellipse, so the orbit in field space
\phi
is an ellipse whose size scale as
a^{-3/2}
, i.e. an ellipse that spirals towards the origin as illustrated in Fig.
24
or by
our animation.
. The parameter
E_{u}
is related to the orbit eccentricity and to the previously defined parameter
0<\epsilon\leq 2
in Eq. (
8.3
)

\displaystyle m^{2}\phi^{2}~=~\rho_{\Phi}~=~mn_{\theta}E_{u}~~~~\Rightarrow~~~~\frac{1}{E_{u}}~=~\frac{n_{\theta}}{m\phi^{2}}~=~\frac{\epsilon}{2}~~~~\Rightarrow~~~~\epsilon~=~2/E_{u}.

(G.21)

For
E_{u}=1
or
\epsilon=2
, the orbit is essentially circular, while for large
E_{u}
or
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
the orbit has a large eccentricity and starts to resemble the trajectory of a real scalar field.

We can calculate the average angular speed

\displaystyle\dot{\theta}~=~\frac{m}{u^{2}}~=~\frac{m}{(E_{u}^{2}-1)^{1/2}\sin(2\tau+\psi)+E_{u}},

(G.22)

by averaging over a time
T=n\pi
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, which is larger than the oscillation period but less than the Hubble time scale

\displaystyle\left<\dot{\theta}\right>~=~\frac{1}{n\pi}\int^{n\pi}_{0}d\tau\frac{m}{(E_{u}^{2}-1)^{1/2}\sin(2\tau+\psi)+E_{u}}~=~m,

(G.23)

where we have integrated an elliptic integral

♠
♠\spadesuit32

♠
♠\spadesuit32

\spadesuit
32

We can freely choose the phase
\psi
such that the sine becomes a cosine and the elliptic integral reads

\displaystyle\int^{2\pi}_{0}\frac{dx}{a+b\cos x}~=~\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{a^{2}-b^{2}}}.

.
We find that the averaged angular velocities becomes independent of the initial value
\theta_{\rm ini}
in Eq. (
G.9
) and converges towards an attractor solution. This is confirmed by the numerical integration of the equations of motion in Fig.
26
.

Exact solution (bis) : quartic potential.

Even if we don’t use it in our work, it may be useful for further studies (of axion dark matter for example
[
80
]
) to give the analog of Eq. (
G.20
) for a quartic potential.
When adding a quartic term to the potential
V~=~\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\lambda\phi^{4}
, the Eq. (
G.15
) becomes

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~~~u^{\prime\prime}+u+\frac{4\alpha}{a^{3}}u^{3}-\frac{1}{u^{3}}~=~0,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha\equiv\lambda Y/(4m^{3}).

(G.24)

We now consider
\phi\gg m/\sqrt{\lambda}
, such that the linear term
u
corresponding to
\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\phi^{2}
can be dropped and Eq. (
G.24
) becomes

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime\prime}+4\alpha\nu^{3}-\frac{1}{\nu^{3}}~=~0\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{1}{2}\nu^{\prime 2}~=~E_{\nu}-V_{\nu}~=~2E_{\nu}-2\alpha\nu^{4}-\frac{1}{\nu^{2}},

(G.25)

with
\nu\equiv a^{-1/2}u
and with
′
denoting the derivative with respect to the conformal time
\eta
(
mdt=d\tau=ad\eta
) and where we have dropped Hubble depending terms. The motion can be interpreted as a scalar
\nu
oscillating in a potential

V_{\nu}=\alpha\nu^{4}+1/(2\nu^{2}),

with the constant of motion

E_{\nu}~=~\nu^{\prime 2}/2+V_{\nu}.

Introducing the new variables
V\equiv-\nu^{2}/\sqrt{2}
and
s\equiv\eta\sqrt{\alpha}
, we deduce

\displaystyle\left(\frac{dV}{ds}\right)^{2}~=~4V^{3}-\frac{4E_{\nu}}{\alpha}V-\frac{2}{\alpha}.

(G.26)

It is known that the
Weierstrass elliptic function

\wp(s;~g_{2},~g_{3})
solves the differential equation of the form
[
274
]

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d\wp(s)}{ds}\right)^{2}~=~4\wp^{3}(s)-g_{2}\wp(s)-g_{3}.

(G.27)

Therefore, Eq. (
G.26
) has a solution as an elliptic orbit described by
\wp(s;~4E_{\nu}/\alpha,~2/\alpha)
.
Applying one of the properties of
\wp
, namely

\wp(u;g_{2},g_{3})~=~k^{2}~\wp(ku;~g_{2}/k^{4},~g_{3}/k^{6}),

the exact solution to Eq. (
G.25
) reads

\displaystyle\nu^{2}~

\displaystyle=~-\sqrt{2}V~=~-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\alpha}\wp(\eta;~4\alpha E_{\nu},~2\alpha^{2}).

(G.28)

Virial theorem.

The Virial theorem in classical mechanics is a well-known tool, e.g.
[
275
]
, for studying the averaged behavior of a stable system. We now use it to study the dynamics of a complex scalar field in a central potential during the early universe.
The EOM in Eq. (
7.16
) and (
7.17
) can be written in terms of kinetic
K
and potential
V
energy densities as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(K+V)~

\displaystyle=~\dot{\phi}\left(\ddot{\phi}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}+\phi\dot{\theta}^{2}+\frac{\phi^{2}}{\dot{\phi}}\dot{\theta}\ddot{\theta}\right)+\dot{T}\frac{\partial V}{\partial T},

(G.29)

\displaystyle=~-(3H+\Gamma)\dot{\phi}^{2}-3H\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}+\dot{T}\frac{\partial V}{\partial T},

(G.30)

\displaystyle=~-(6H+2\Gamma)K_{\phi}-6HK_{\theta}+\dot{T}\frac{\partial V}{\partial T},

(G.31)

where
K_{\phi}\equiv\dot{\phi}^{2}/2
and
K_{\theta}\equiv\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}/2
are the kinetic energy in radial and angular modes, respectively.
After
3H\simeq m_{r,\textrm{eff}}
, the field oscillates fast compared to the expansion and damping rate. The average over many field cycles but over a time shorter than the
H^{-1}
reads

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left<K+V\right>~

\displaystyle=~-(6H+2\Gamma)\left<K_{\phi}\right>-6H\left<K_{\theta}\right>+\dot{T}\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}.

(G.32)

We introduce the
Virial
parameter
G
, e.g.
[
275
]

\displaystyle G~\equiv~\sum_{i=\phi,\theta}p_{i}\cdot r_{i}~=~\dot{\phi}\phi+\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}\theta,

(G.33)

where
r_{\phi}=\phi
and
r_{\theta}=\theta
, and
p_{i}\equiv\partial\mathcal{L}/\partial r_{i}
.
By taking its time derivative, we deduce

\displaystyle\frac{dG}{dt}~

\displaystyle=~\dot{\phi}^{2}+\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}+\phi\ddot{\phi}+\phi^{2}\theta\left(\ddot{\theta}+2\frac{\dot{\phi}}{\phi}\dot{\theta}\right),

(G.34)

\displaystyle=~\dot{\phi}^{2}+\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}-\phi\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}-(3H+\Gamma)\phi\dot{\phi}-3H\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}\theta,

(G.35)

\displaystyle\simeq~2K-\phi\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi},

(G.36)

where we used Eq. (
7.16
) and (
7.17
) in the second step, and where we neglected the damping terms in the last step.
The Virial theorem states that whenever the system is stable, the averaged kinetic and potential energies are related

\displaystyle\left<\frac{dG}{dt}\right>~=~\lim_{\Delta T\rightarrow\infty}\frac{G(T+\Delta T)-G(T)}{\Delta T}~=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\left<2K\right>~=~\left<\phi\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}\right>.

(G.37)

which resembles the Virial theorem for a real scalar
[
193
,
194
]
.
For convenience, we consider the field behavior before the damping term becomes effective and deduces the average EOM from Eq. (
G.32
) and (
G.37
)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left<K+V\right>~=~-6H\left<K\right>+\dot{T}\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{d}{dt}\left<\phi\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}+2V\right>~=~-6H\left<\phi\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}\right>+\dot{T}\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}.

(G.38)

Monomial potential.

From plugging the potential
V~\propto~\phi^{n}
into Eq. (
G.38
), we obtain

\displaystyle(n+2)\frac{d}{dt}\left<V\right>~=~-6Hn\left<V\right>\hskip 10.00002pt\implies\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d}{dt}\left<\rho_{\Phi}\right>~=~-\frac{6n}{2+n}H\left<\rho\right>\hskip 10.00002pt\implies\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{d\ln\left<\rho_{\Phi}\right>}{d\ln a}~=~-\frac{6n}{2+n},

(G.39)

with
\left<\rho_{\Phi}\right>
is the average total energy density of the field

\left<\rho_{\Phi}\right>=\left<K+V\right>=(2+n)\left<V\right>/2.

(G.40)

We deduce the redshift laws of
\left<\rho_{\Phi}\right>
and
\left<\phi\right>

\displaystyle\left<\rho_{\Phi}\right>~

\displaystyle\propto~a^{-\frac{6n}{2+n}},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\left<\phi\right>~\propto~a^{-\frac{6}{2+n}}.

(G.41)

For the quadratic and quartic potentials, the complex scalar field behaves like matter (
\rho_{\Phi}\propto a^{-3}
,
\phi\propto a^{-3/2}
) and radiation (
\rho_{\Phi}\propto a^{-4}
,
\phi\propto a^{-1}
), respectively. The scaling
\rho_{\Phi}\propto a^{-3}
is confirmed by the exact solution in Eq. (
G.18
). We show that, cf. Eq. (
G.54
), that Eq. (
G.39
) holds without any average after radial damping
\dot{\phi}\to 0
.

Thermal mass.

From plugging the potential
V=\lambda T^{2}\phi^{2}/2
into Eq. (
G.38
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left<\phi^{2}\right>~=~-3H\left<\phi^{2}\right>-\frac{\dot{T}}{T}\left<\phi^{2}\right>\hskip 10.00002pt\implies\hskip 10.00002pt\phi^{2}\propto a^{-3}T^{-1}\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{and}\hskip 20.00003ptV\propto a^{-3}T.

(G.42)

We conclude that in a radiation-dominated universe, a scalar field dominated by its thermal mass redshifts like radiation.

G.3
The radial damping

Amount of rotation.

The parameter
\epsilon
expresses the amount of rotation generated by the explicit breaking at the time of oscillation
t_{\rm osc}
, cf. Eq. (
8.3
)

\displaystyle\epsilon~=~\frac{\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}/2}{V(\phi)/m_{r,\mathrm{eff}}},

(G.43)

From Eq. (
8.9
), we deduce that
\epsilon
becomes a conserved quantity through Hubble expansion
d\epsilon/da=0
, as soon as
V(\phi)
become dominated by its quadratic term.
It can be rewritten in terms of the ratio between energy densities as

\displaystyle\epsilon~=~\left(\frac{\rho_{\theta}}{V}\right)\left(\frac{m_{r,\mathrm{eff}}}{\dot{\theta}}\right),\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\rho_{\theta}\equiv\dot{\theta^{2}}\phi^{2}/2.

(G.44)

Since
\left<\dot{\theta}\right>=m_{r,\textrm{eff}}
, see Fig.
26
, we deduce the rotational energy density to be an
\epsilon
fraction of the potential energy density

\displaystyle\rho_{\theta}~=~\epsilon V(\phi).

(G.45)

Note that
\epsilon\leq 1
from preventing the field to spin upward.

\begin{cases}\epsilon=\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}/(2\rho_{r}(\phi)/m_{r,\textrm{eff}}),\vskip 14.22636pt\\
\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}=\textrm{constant},\vskip 14.22636pt\\
\dot{\theta}_{\rm after}=m_{r,\textrm{eff}},\end{cases}\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\theta}\Big|_{\rm after}=\epsilon\rho_{r}(\phi)

(G.46)

Drop of energy density during radial damping.

During the radial damping
\dot{\phi}\to 0
, see Sec.
7.6
, the elliptic orbits becomes a circular one. During this process, the scalar field kinetic energy is damped but the rotational kinetic energy is preserved. Therefore, after the radial damping the total energy density of the complex scalar field drops by

\displaystyle\rho_{\Phi}^{\mathrm{after}}~=~\epsilon\,\rho_{\Phi}^{\mathrm{before}}\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\phi^{2}_{\mathrm{after}}~=~\epsilon\,\phi^{2}_{\mathrm{before}},

(G.47)

where the second equation assumes the quadratic potential.

\phi_{\mathrm{before}}
is the field value just before thermalization, and it can be computed from Eq. (
G.41
)

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{\Phi}^{\mathrm{osc}}}{\rho_{\Phi}^{\mathrm{before}}}~=~\left(\frac{a_{\textrm{damp}}}{a_{\textrm{osc}}}\right)^{3}~=~\left(\frac{\phi_{\textrm{osc}}}{\phi_{\textrm{before}}}\right)^{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\phi_{\textrm{before}}~=~\frac{(\rho_{\Phi}^{\mathrm{before}})^{1/2}}{m_{r,\textrm{eff}}(\phi_{\textrm{osc}})},

(G.48)

where we used that the energy density just after oscillation can be written as
\rho_{\Phi}^{\mathrm{osc}}=\phi_{\textrm{osc}}^{2}m_{\textrm{eff}}(\phi_{\rm osc})^{2}
.
In Fig.
59
, we show that the result in Eq.(
G.47
) is confirmed by numerical integration of the equations of motion.

Figure 59:

Numerical integration of the equations of motion in Eqs. (
7.16
), (
7.17
) and (
7.18
). The energy density (
left
) and the radial value (
right
) of the complex scalar field drops by a factor
\epsilon
after the radial damping. The larger
\epsilon
, the larger the rotational energy density produced during the initial kick and the smaller the energy drop during the radial damping. The extreme case
\epsilon=1
corresponds to a trajectory which is already circular right after the initial angular kick.

Impact on kination duration.

We now study the impact of the energy drop in Eq. (
G.47
) on the duration of the kination era.
As shown in Fig.
27
, we must consider two possible scenarios, depending on whether the radial damping occurs before or after the scalar domination. For the sake of simplicity we assume an instantaneous drop in energy density at the time of radial damping.

•

Damping before domination.
The energy transferred to the thermal bath is negligible. However, the
\epsilon
factor in the energy density in Eq. (
G.47
) delays the time when the scalar field dominates the energy density of the universe

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}_{\mathrm{dom}}~=~\epsilon^{4}\left.\frac{\rho_{\Phi}^{4}}{\rho_{\mathrm{rad}}^{3}}\right|_{\mathrm{at~oscillation}}~=~\epsilon^{4}\rho_{\mathrm{dom}},

(G.49)

where
\rho_{\mathrm{dom}}
is the domination energy scale without energy drop during damping (case
\epsilon=1
) and where we have used Eq. (
G.79
).

As a consequence of the energy drop, the kination era ends earlier and is then shorter

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{KD},f}~=~\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{KD},i}^{2}}{\rho^{\prime}_{\mathrm{dom}}}~=~\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{KD},i}^{2}}{\rho_{\mathrm{dom}}}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)^{4}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{a_{\mathrm{KD},f}}{a_{\mathrm{KD},i}}~=~\left(\frac{\rho^{\prime}_{\mathrm{dom}}}{\rho_{\mathrm{KD},i}}\right)^{1/6}~=~\left(\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{dom}}}{\rho_{\mathrm{KD},i}}\right)^{1/6}\epsilon^{2/3}.

(G.50)

•

Damping after domination.
A substantial amount of energy is injected in the thermal bath.
For
\epsilon=\mathcal{O}(0.1)
, the radial damping reduces the scalar energy by a factor
\mathcal{O}(10)
, so that the produced SM radiation dominates the energy density.
Later, the domination of the energy density by the rotating scalar generates a second matter era at

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}_{\mathrm{dom}}~=~\epsilon^{4}\rho_{\mathrm{damp}},

(G.51)

The scale of kination ending and its duration are given by Eq. (
G.50
) after replacing
\rho_{\mathrm{dom}}
by
\rho_{\mathrm{damp}}

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{KD},f}~=~\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{KD},i}^{2}}{\rho_{\mathrm{damp}}}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)^{4}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{a_{\mathrm{KD},f}}{a_{\mathrm{KD},i}}~=~\left(\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{damp}}}{\rho_{\mathrm{KD},i}}\right)^{1/6}\epsilon^{2/3}.

(G.52)

Note that the presence of the radiation era in between the two matter eras could leave a distinctive imprint in the SGWB.

Too small
\epsilon
kills the kination era.

In Eqs. (
G.50
) and (
G.52
), we have seen that the kination duration receives a suppression factor
\epsilon^{2/3}
. This implies the existence of a lower bound on
\epsilon
below which no kination era is generated

\displaystyle\mathrm{Requiring}~~~~\frac{a_{\mathrm{KD},f}}{a_{\mathrm{KD},i}}\geq 1~~~~\Longrightarrow~~~~\epsilon\geq\left[\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{KD},i}}{\max(\rho_{\mathrm{dom}},\rho_{\mathrm{damp}})}\right],

(G.53)

for both damping before and after the scalar domination. In Sec.
9
, we show that the scenario II, in which thermal corrections to the potential are present at the onset of the radial mode oscillation, necessarily predicts a value of
\epsilon
smaller than Eq. (
G.53
).

G.4
After the radial damping

In this section, we reproduce the results from
[
38
]
and derive the evolution of the radial
\phi
and angular
\dot{\theta}
field values, their energy density
\rho_{\Phi}
and equation of state
\omega_{\Phi}
, in an arbitrary
U(1)
-symmetric potential
V(\phi)
, after that the radial mode has been damped
\dot{\phi}/\phi\ll\sqrt{V^{{}^{\prime\prime}}}
.

Radial evolution.

In the limit
\dot{\phi}\to 0
, the radial EOM in Eq. (
7.16
) takes a simple form

\displaystyle\dot{\theta}^{2}\phi~=~\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\dot{\theta}^{2}~=~2\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}},

(G.54)

We inject it in the equation of conservation of
n_{\theta}=\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}

\displaystyle\frac{d(a^{3}n_{\theta})}{da}~

\displaystyle=~3a^{2}\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}+2a^{3}\phi\dot{\theta}\frac{d\phi}{da}+a^{3}\phi^{2}\frac{d\dot{\theta}}{da},

(G.55)

\displaystyle\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt0~

\displaystyle=~3a^{2}\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}+2a^{3}\phi\dot{\theta}\frac{d\phi}{da}+a^{3}\phi^{2}\frac{4\phi}{2\dot{\theta}}\cdot\frac{d\phi}{da}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}},

(G.56)

\displaystyle\implies\hskip 20.00003pt~a\frac{d\phi}{da}~

\displaystyle=~\frac{-3\phi\dot{\theta}^{2}}{2\dot{\theta}^{2}+2\phi^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}}~=~\frac{-3\phi\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}}{2\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}+\phi^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}},

(G.57)

From which we obtain

\frac{d\ln\phi}{d\ln a}~=~\frac{-3\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}}{2\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}+\phi^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}}.

(G.58)

Angular evolution.

Starting from Eq.(
G.54
), we can write

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln\dot{\theta}^{2}}{d\ln a}~=~2\frac{a}{\dot{\theta}^{2}}\frac{d\phi^{2}}{da}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}~=~4\frac{\phi^{2}}{\dot{\theta}^{2}}\frac{d\ln\phi}{d\ln a}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}~=~2\frac{\phi^{2}}{\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}}\frac{d\ln\phi}{d\ln a}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}},

(G.59)

where
\frac{d\ln\phi}{d\ln a}
is given by Eq. (
G.58
).

Energy density evolution.

After radial damping, the kinetic energy of the radial mode vanishes such that the energy density of the complex field reads

\displaystyle\rho_{\Phi}~=~\frac{1}{2}\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}+V(\phi)~=~\phi^{2}\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}+V(\phi).

(G.60)

Taking the derivative with respect to
\phi
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{d\rho_{\Phi}}{d\phi}~=~2\phi\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}+2\phi^{3}\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi}~=~2\phi\left(2\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}+\phi^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}\right).

(G.61)

Now taking the derivative with respect to
a

\displaystyle a\frac{d\rho_{\Phi}}{da}~=~\frac{d\rho_{\Phi}}{d\phi}\cdot a\frac{d\phi}{da}~=~\left[2\phi\left(2\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}+\phi^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}\right)\right]\left[\frac{-3\phi\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}}{2\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}+\phi^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}}\right]~=~-6\phi^{2}\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}~\equiv~-6\rho_{\theta},

(G.62)

where
\rho_{\theta}
is the kinetic energy density of the angular field

\displaystyle\rho_{\theta}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}~=~\phi^{2}\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}},

(G.63)

we deduce

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln\rho_{\Phi}}{d\ln a}~=~\frac{-6\phi^{2}\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}}{\phi^{2}\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}+V}.

(G.64)

Equation of state.

Using
\dot{\phi}\to 0
and Eq. (
G.54
), the equation of state of the complex scalar field becomes

♠
♠\spadesuit33

♠
♠\spadesuit33

\spadesuit
33

As a sanity check, we can use

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}+3H(\rho+P)~=~0~\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{d\ln\rho}{d\ln a}~=~-3(1+\omega),

(G.65)

to show that Eq. (
G.64
) and Eq. (
G.66
) are consistent with each others.

\displaystyle\omega_{\Phi}~=~\frac{\frac{1}{2}\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}-V}{\frac{1}{2}\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}+V}~=~\frac{\phi^{2}\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}-V}{\phi^{2}\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}+V}.

(G.66)

Example: Nearly-quadratic potential.

This is the scenario considered in this work. The potential and its derivatives read

♠
♠\spadesuit34

♠
♠\spadesuit34

\spadesuit
34

Note that is the minimum of potential is not vanishing
V_{\textrm{min}}\neq 0
, then the evolution of the energy density and EOS becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln\rho}{d\ln a}~=~\frac{-6\log\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f^{2}}\right)}{2\log\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f^{2}}\right)-1+\frac{f^{2}}{\phi^{2}}+\frac{V_{\textrm{min}}}{m^{2}f^{2}}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\omega_{\Phi}=\frac{\phi^{2}-f_{a}^{2}}{2\phi^{2}\log{\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}}-f_{a}^{2}+\phi^{2}-V_{\textrm{min}}/f_{a}^{2}}.

(G.67)

In order to generated a kination EOS, in this work we assume
V_{\textrm{min}}\ll m^{2}f^{2}
.

\displaystyle V~=~m^{2}\phi^{2}\left[\log\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f^{2}}\right)-1\right]+m^{2}f^{2},\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}~=~m^{2}\log\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f^{2}}\right),\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}~=~\frac{m^{2}}{\phi^{2}}.

(G.68)

From using Eq. (
G.58
) and (
G.59
), we deduce the evolution of the radial and angular component of the scalar field after radial damping

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln\phi}{d\ln a}~=~\frac{-3\log\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f^{2}}\right)}{2\log\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f^{2}}\right)+1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{d\ln\dot{\theta}^{2}}{d\ln a}~=~\frac{-6}{2\log\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f^{2}}\right)+1}.

(G.69)

Using Eq. (
G.64
) and (
G.66
), we deduce the evolution of the complex scalar field energy density
\rho_{\Phi}
and its equation of state
\omega_{\Phi}

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln\rho_{\Phi}}{d\ln a}~=~\frac{-6\log\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f^{2}}\right)}{2\log\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{f^{2}}\right)-1+\frac{f^{2}}{\phi^{2}}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\omega_{\Phi}=\frac{\phi^{2}-f_{a}^{2}}{2\phi^{2}\log{\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}}-f_{a}^{2}+\phi^{2}}.

(G.70)

For
\phi\gg f_{a}
we have

\displaystyle\phi~\propto~a^{-3/2},\hskip 20.00003pt\dot{\theta}~\propto~a^{0},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\Phi}~\propto~a^{-3},\hskip 20.00003pt\omega_{\Phi}~\simeq~0,

(G.71)

and for
\phi\simeq f_{a}
we have

\displaystyle\phi~\propto~a^{0},\hskip 20.00003pt\dot{\theta}~\propto~a^{-3},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\Phi}~\propto~a^{-6},\hskip 20.00003pt\omega_{\Phi}~\simeq~1.

(G.72)

Example: quartic potential.

For the sake of the comparison, we consider the potential

\displaystyle V~=~\lambda^{2}\left(\phi^{2}-f^{2}\right)^{2},\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{\partial V}{\partial\phi^{2}}~=~2\lambda^{2}\left(\phi^{2}-f^{2}\right),\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{\partial^{2}V}{(\partial\phi^{2})^{2}}~

\displaystyle=~2\lambda^{2}.

(G.73)

Eq. (
G.58
) and (
G.59
)
From using Eq. (
G.58
) and (
G.59
), we deduce the evolution of the radial and angular component of the scalar field after radial damping

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln\phi}{d\ln a}~=~\frac{-3\left[\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}-1\right]}{3\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}-2}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{d\ln\dot{\theta}^{2}}{d\ln a}~=~\frac{-6\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}}{3\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}-1}.

(G.74)

Using Eq. (
G.64
) and (
G.66
), we deduce the evolution of the complex scalar field energy density
\rho_{\Phi}
and equation of state
\omega_{\Phi}

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln\rho_{\Phi}}{d\ln a}~=~\frac{-12\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}}{3\frac{\phi^{2}}{f_{a}^{2}}-1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\omega_{\Phi}=\frac{\phi^{2}+f_{a}^{2}}{3\phi^{2}-f_{a}^{2}}.

(G.75)

For
\phi\gg f_{a}
we have

\displaystyle\phi~\propto~a^{-1},\hskip 20.00003pt\dot{\theta}~\propto~a^{-1},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\Phi}~\propto~a^{-4},\hskip 20.00003pt\omega_{\Phi}~\simeq~\frac{1}{3},

(G.76)

and for
\phi\simeq f_{a}
we have

\displaystyle\phi~\propto~a^{0},\hskip 20.00003pt\dot{\theta}~\propto~a^{-3},\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\Phi}~\propto~a^{-6},\hskip 20.00003pt\omega_{\Phi}~\simeq~1.

(G.77)

The evolution of
\rho_{\Phi}
and
\omega_{\Phi}
in nearly-quadratic and quartic potentials are shown in Fig.
60
. Only the matter phase induced by the nearly-quadratic potential can allow the initially sub-dominant
\Phi
to dominate the energy density of the universe and, later, generate a kination-dominated era. For this reason, in this work we focus on a nearly-quadratic potential. In Fig.
28
, we show the evolution of
\phi
,
\dot{\theta}
,
\rho_{\Phi}
and
\omega_{\Phi}
in the nearly-quadratic potential, obtained after numerically integrating the equations of motion in Eqs. (
7.16
), (
7.17
) and (
7.18
).

Figure 60:

Evolutions of the energy density (
left
) and the equation-of-state (
right
) of the complex scalar field, rotating in a nearly-quadratic or quartic potential after its radial motion has been damped. Both cases lead to a kination EOS when
\phi\to f_{a}
. For
\phi\gg f_{a}
, the quartic potential gives a radiation EOS while the quadratic potential gives a matter EOS

G.5
Derivation of the cosmological-history relations

In this appendix, we derive the expression used in Sec.
8.2
, neglecting the factor
(\epsilon/2)
, and for which we refer to App.
G.3
.

Matter domination.

After the field starts oscillating at
3H_{\rm osc}=m_{r,\textrm{eff}}
, it redshifts like matter and induces a matter-domination era as soon as it dominates the energy density of the universe when

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm dom}~\equiv~V(\phi_{\rm ini})\left(\frac{a_{\textrm{osc}}}{a_{\rm dom}}\right)^{3}~\simeq~3H_{\rm osc}^{2}M_{\rm pl}^{2}\left(\frac{a_{\textrm{osc}}}{a_{\rm dom}}\right)^{4},

(G.78)

where we have neglected the change of relativistic degrees of freedom in the thermal bath.
We obtain

\displaystyle\frac{a_{\rm dom}}{a_{\textrm{osc}}}~\simeq~\frac{M_{{\rm Pl}}^{2}m_{\textrm{eff}}^{2}(\phi_{\textrm{ini}})}{3V(\phi_{\textrm{ini}})}\hskip 20.00003pt\textrm{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\rm dom}~=~\frac{27V^{4}(\phi_{\textrm{ini}})}{M_{{\rm Pl}}^{6}m_{\textrm{eff}}^{6}(\phi_{\textrm{ini}})}.

(G.79)

Radial damping.

The decay of the radial mode, occuring at the rate
\Gamma
, dissipates the radial kinetic energy when

\Gamma~\simeq~H\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\rm damp}~=~3M_{{\rm Pl}}^{2}\Gamma^{2},

(G.80)

after that the universe has expanded by

\displaystyle\frac{a_{\rm damp}}{a_{\rm dom}}~=~\left(\frac{\rho_{\rm dom}}{\rho_{\rm damp}}\right)^{1/3}~=~\left[\frac{9V^{4}(\phi_{\textrm{ini}})}{M_{{\rm Pl}}^{8}\Gamma^{2}m_{\textrm{eff}}^{6}(\phi_{\textrm{ini}})}\right]^{1/3}.

(G.81)

Start of kination (starting).

The scalar field reaches the kination-liked equation of state when the radial field-value settles down to its final VEV
\phi\rightarrow f_{a}
, when the energy density is

\rho_{\textrm{KD},i}~=~\cancel{\frac{1}{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}+V(\phi\rightarrow f_{a})\simeq~\frac{1}{2}\phi^{2}\dot{\theta}^{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\implies\hskip 20.00003pt\rho_{\textrm{KD},i}~\simeq~\frac{1}{2}f^{2}m_{\textrm{eff}}^{2}(f),

(G.82)

where the radial kinetic energy vanishes after
H<\Gamma
and where we assume the vacuum energy to vanish at the minimum.
The duration of the matter era reads

\displaystyle\frac{a_{\textrm{KD},i}}{\textrm{min}(a_{\textrm{\rm dom}},~a_{\rm damp})}~=~\left(\frac{\rho_{\rm damp}}{\rho_{\textrm{KD}}}\right)^{1/3}.

(G.83)

End of kination.

The period of kination lasts until the energy density of the scalar field drops below that of thermal bath.
The value of the radiation energy density depends on whether entropy has been injected during the damping of the radial mode. This depends on whether radial damping has occured after domination
\rho_{\rm damp}<\rho_{\rm dom}
, or not. Its energy density at the end of kination era follows the scaling law

\displaystyle\rho_{\textrm{rad}}~=~\rho_{\textrm{KD},f}~=~\begin{cases}\rho_{\rm dom}\left(\frac{a_{\rm dom}}{a_{\textrm{KD},f}}\right)^{4},~~~\qquad\qquad\text{if}~\rho_{\rm damp}>\rho_{\rm dom},\\[7.5pt]
\rho_{\rm damp}\left(\frac{a_{\rm damp}}{a_{\textrm{KD},f}}\right)^{4},\qquad\qquad\text{if}~\rho_{\rm damp}<\rho_{\rm dom}.\\
\end{cases}

(G.84)

Similarly, the scalar field which has undergone a period of matter folowed by a period of kination has the energy density

\displaystyle\rho_{\Phi}~=~\rho_{\textrm{KD},f}~=~\begin{cases}\rho_{\rm dom}\left(\frac{a_{\rm dom}}{a_{\textrm{KD},i}}\right)^{3}\left(\frac{a_{\textrm{KD},i}}{a_{\textrm{KD},f}}\right)^{6},~~~\qquad\qquad\text{if}~\rho_{\rm damp}>\rho_{\rm dom},\\[7.5pt]
\rho_{\rm damp}\left(\frac{a_{\rm damp}}{a_{\textrm{KD},i}}\right)^{3}\left(\frac{a_{\textrm{KD},i}}{a_{\textrm{KD},f}}\right)^{6},\qquad\qquad\text{if}~\rho_{\rm damp}<\rho_{\rm dom}.\\
\end{cases}

(G.85)

From the above two equations, we deduce the energy density at the end of kination
\rho_{\textrm{KD},f}

\rho_{\textrm{KD},f}~=~\begin{cases}\frac{\rho_{\rm KD,i}^{2}}{\rho_{\rm dom}},~\qquad\qquad\text{if}~\rho_{\rm damp}>\rho_{\rm dom},\\[7.5pt]
\frac{\rho_{\rm KD,i}^{2}}{\rho_{\rm damp}},\qquad\qquad\text{if}~\rho_{\rm damp}<\rho_{\rm dom},\\
\end{cases}

(G.86)

and the duration of the kination era

\displaystyle\frac{a_{\textrm{KD},f}}{a_{\textrm{KD},i}}~=~\begin{cases}\left(\frac{\rho_{\rm dom}}{\rho_{\textrm{KD},i}}\right)^{1/6},~\qquad\qquad\text{if}~\rho_{\rm damp}>\rho_{\rm dom},\\[7.5pt]
\left(\frac{\rho_{\rm damp}}{\rho_{\textrm{KD},i}}\right)^{1/6},\qquad\qquad\text{if}~\rho_{\rm damp}<\rho_{\rm dom}.\end{cases}

(G.87)
